> I appreciate this class less as a metaphysical roadmap and more as a disciplined exercise in self-awareness. I don’t share the view that there’s a benevolent spiritual substrate or a hidden divine order beneath reality, but I do think the Great Books are invaluable for forcing us to confront how human beings actually think, imagine, rationalize, and delude themselves. For me, the “enslavement” isn’t cosmic or imposed by a false reality—it’s rooted in our nature as social animals with evolved instincts for conformity, hierarchy, and meaning-making. Still, reading these texts seriously is one of the best ways to wake up to those constraints, even if one ultimately rejects the spiritual conclusions. In that sense, I see this course as useful not for revealing ultimate truths, but for sharpening clarity about the human condition.

> So it’s time to turn that examination inwards. Why are we so unhappy about his attitude, or when the student doesn’t get it? Do our egos get in the way of thinking in those moments, and why does that Chinese student misunderstand things the way he does?  
> I don’t think there must be some short and snappy answer to these questions. It’s not about being effectively judgmental. But when something causes a ripple in our feelings, I think it’s a great thing to meditate on. The balance is tricky between self-reflection and rumination, especially when we resist change.  
> And I think we’re really good at that resistance to change, unless life gives us some massive setbacks and breaks the shell of self-assurance that our societies condition into us.  
>   
> Edit:  
> And there is one thing that I want to say here about Jiang.   
> You can see my reply above emphasizing personal agency. But when Jiang talks about everything in society being a lie, and including LLMs/AI being a trap to enslave the mind - I think an argument I want to make is that, we should look at Jiang's response to one of his high school students; this is not the first time he got confusion and lack of understanding from his students. One of the girls in his class Amber is really thoughtful and was able to keep up with some of his concepts and ask interesting questions, but that isn't the norm among 2 other boys in his class. And I think that norm should actually be the focus.  
>   
> Jiang is too far removed from reality, his jumps in logic and intuition too quick for the mostly concrete minds of high schoolers who are still struggling with abstraction. Society is the unavoidable and unfortunately necessary experience that Jiang himself had to go through to gain his insights. Although I want to interject here that Jiang can't throw all of his elaborations into his lectures, and he's oversimplifying everything he teaches for high schoolers.   
>   
> AI is merely an acceleration of the modern phenomena of mental entrapment, and I want to argue that the faster you fall for then break away from AI, the more awareness of yourself you can gain. It's an accelerated course of modernity, a pattern engine that compresses human thought on the web into a computed representation.   
> Hell, even he himself uses ChatGPT for summaries. For him it's not hypocrisy, it's just tool use, but for high schoolers it might seem he contradicts himself. His lectures are the kind of things that his students will question and shrug off and then in their late 20s or early 30s dawn on them that - oh, that's what he meant.  
>

> @vivekranavaya4043 I agree. You can be skeptical about anything, including the skepticism itself. I am often skeptical of my own thoughts as well. But as you say, skepticism alone is probably not that productive and can lead to all kinds of issues, like overthinking or analysis paralysis.  
>   
> I don't think one should be skeptical of everything and believe in nothing though. It's fine to believe in things and if you want to believe everything Jiang says that's fine. I just think it's good to question things from time to time and to think for yourself, do your own research etc.  
>   
> It can be helpful to have a framework to hold onto and I don't think people who spread certain ideas necessarily have malicious intent. Certain ideas are spread for a reason, often because people think they are useful or have some value to them. If people get value out of Jiang's ideas that's good for them.  
>   
> But that doesn't mean that those are necessarily "true" in some objective sense. What I mostly have an issue with is people who present certain ideas as if they are facts of reality, as opposed to just a product of their subjective experience. You don't have to preface everything you say with "in my opinion..." or anything, but at least don't be like "this is how things are" when you can't back those things up, especially if it's about abstract things like spirituality.

### **@bottomethgear9622** | 👍 31 likes

This is good as always, but there's an important flaw to address in this frame of "leaving the fake world" or "rejecting the lie".

As mentioned in multiple videos, the higher and lower vibrations of the universe is in the same continuum. This means that they are strongly coupled towards each other.

If you truly listen to your heart, there's a reason why the idea is scary, or why you're still afraid of dying. And it's not inherently bad to be scared of death. Because the truth is, the body matters, not just the soul. The will of the monad needs a medium to enact itself towards the lower vibration. Or maybe, the medium itself is the manifestation of the lower vibration of the monad.

From what I see, rejecting the lower vibration means rejecting a part of the monad itself, disrupting the continuum of the vibrations. The lie is a part of the truth. While the reckless chasing of the truth is a part of the lie. The lie that we're concerned about now is the notion that the lie, or the lower vibration is "all there is".

If we go back to the cave analogy, accepting the truth means that we acknowledge that the cave is not all there is. But at the same time, acknowledging that the cave exists as a part of the world. This has been mentioned in the other videos that if we "surfaced" and we still have other people in the "cave" (lower vibration), we would still not be fully happy. In fact, there's this phenomenon in psychology (dr. K addressed this in one of his vids) called the "deep hurt". I believe that this is the same phenomenon that's being described. And to hoist the others from the cave, we HAVE to resonate with the "lower frequencies".

Heaven on earth is fulfilled through integration. A full, unimpeded wave energy transfer throughout the whole continuum, from high to low frequencies. We can start with a leap of faith, but achieving continuity (building a bridge/taking down the wall) between the materium and immaterium is the crucial thing to do.

### **@thepointedhat** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TsD-8FGA84A&lc=UgyNg5H7HkjE3P3Jimh4AaABAg)

I would like to recommend Terry Pratchett's Discworld as part of the Great Books to learn about life. Discworld is a satire fantasy of our world, which makes it funny and gives you a lot to think about. Almost any subject you can imagine is considered from a philosophical standpoint in Terry's books. In this class two examples came to mind from the Tiffany Aching series. This is a story of a young girl who becomes one of the most powerful witches in the world. In one of her stories she gets a glimpse of the universe 'open your eyes again'. It is too big to keep in your mind all the time and even after we've seen it we can't keep the full concept in mind, but we can feel the vastness and wonder for brief moments. And the Kelda's  (a female clan leader) cauldron, here she can travel through space time and ask questions to all the Keldas from the past and future. I thought it striking how similar these ideas were to some of the ones discussed today. If you need a rest from the modern world but still want to stretch your brain, check out Terry. And note that with every re-read you will find more jokes and a deeper understanding of the context. I hope you consider trying them out.

### **@Rajadhiraja-Kalki** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TsD-8FGA84A&lc=UgyNlFKCrmJVY34HuXh4AaABAg)

The Ghost of the Jesuite Court Astronomers (耶稣会士的幽灵)

Jiang spent years in the Imperial Palace archives (hence his nickname “the man who lives in the Forbidden City after closing time”). He found the original 17th–18th century memorials written by Jesuits (Verbiest, Schall, etc.) who taught the Kangxi and Qianlong emperors Weiqi-based statecraft. Those memorials contain the first explicit articulation of “using 300-year cycles to manage barbarian threats.” Jiang realized the Qing court already thought in exactly these time scales; Mao and Deng simply re-activated an ancient operating system that had been dormant since 1911.



The Two Secret Soviet Masters (苏联两个隐师)

In the 1980s, when almost no one in China had access, Jiang was one of the very few allowed to read the ultra-restricted “Gray Library” in Beijing: captured German and Soviet war-planning documents. Two names almost never mentioned publicly:



Genrikh Lyushkov (NKVD defector who brought Stalin’s 100-year plans for Asia)

Yevgeny Varga (the economist who created Stalin’s “long-cycle capitalist crisis” model)

From them Jiang stole the mathematical formalism of ultra-long planning under conditions of deliberate self-sacrifice (“socialism in one country” = accept 70 years of poverty to outflank capitalism forever).



The Xiangshan Temple Circle (香山寺圈子)

1989–1995. After June 4th, a tiny hand-picked group of theorists was quietly gathered at the Fragrant Hills (Xiangshan) outside Beijing for off-the-record seminars. Present: certain elders, two PLA strategists, one prince of the former imperial house (in disguise), and Jiang. They re-read the entire Twenty-Four Histories at night, line by line, asking only one question: “Which dynasties successfully managed the 150–200 year foreign encroachment cycle and which ones failed?” The answer became the skeleton of the Nine-Round Immortal Game.



The Ultimate Hidden Master: “Mr. H.” (H先生)

This is the one that is literally never spoken aloud.

In the early 1990s a mysterious overseas Chinese billionaire (initial H, Singapore/Malaysian papers) quietly funded Jiang’s private research office for almost a decade. Insiders say “Mr. H.” was not just rich; he was the living heir to the strategic tradition of the old Nanyang Chinese secret societies + British intelligence methods learned during WWII. He allegedly gave Jiang the final missing piece: how to run triple-ledger accounting across centuries (one ledger for the foreigners, one for the Chinese people, one only for the very top).

### **@MonicaLopez-ze3dd**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TsD-8FGA84A&lc=UgzMksDbuOkwSB9CB_J4AaABAg)

Este video introductorio presenta el curso "Great Books #1: Secrets of the Universe" impartido por el profesor Jiang. El argumento principal es que los grandes libros no son solo literatura, sino herramientas para alcanzar el máximo potencial humano al revelar los secretos del universo y de nuestra propia consciencia (0:01-0:20).

Puntos clave del video:

La crítica al sistema educativo tradicional: El profesor Jiang argumenta que la educación convencional promueve una visión puramente materialista y evolucionista, ocultando la naturaleza espiritual del ser humano (0:27-1:07).
Consciencia y Espiritualidad: Se plantea que el universo no es material, sino consciente, y que nosotros tenemos un alma que conecta con lo divino. Los actos fundamentales del ser humano son el amor (fuerza unificadora) y la imaginación (fuerza animadora) (2:16-3:07).
La Alegoría de la Caverna de Platón: Se utiliza este mito para explicar cómo la sociedad nos mantiene esclavizados en una realidad material falsa (la caverna), consumiendo nuestra atención a través de medios, tecnología y entretenimiento (10:02-13:03).
El camino a la libertad (Iluminación): La verdadera libertad consiste en escapar de este sistema, alcanzar la consciencia superior mediante la meditación, el estudio de la geometría sagrada y, a veces, hacks como psicodélicos o experiencias cercanas a la muerte (15:55-22:42).
Christ Consciousness y los Grandes Libros: El objetivo es convertir nuestro cuerpo en un vehículo para la consciencia superior, tal como los cristianos buscan la "consciencia de Cristo". En este curso, se busca integrar las mentes de autores como Homer, Platón, Dante y Kant para alcanzar la inmortalidad y la iluminación (25:35-32:15).

Libros a estudiar en el semestre:
1.  La Ilíada de Homero
2.  La Odisea de Homero
3.  La República de Platón
4.  La Eneida de Virgilio
5.  La Divina Comedia de Dante (43:45-44:15).

Love this topic sooo much, and yes we are raised to be slaves, to money mostly. But doesnt our consciousness grow also out of the contrast that we see in the material world? I was taught the allegory of Plato in university, for example, and combined with religious teachings i had while i was younger did open a door for me into spirituality. So, it is not all bad. Most of us get stuck in the material world, but a lot of us have this yearning for connection  and it is possible to seek it out. I love having found this video, it comes right at a time when i have become more committed to align myself with source. And I have already received backlash from those around, being called crazy and selfish. It has hurt but I know that one needs to shed what does not truly serve you. I am happy to find that there are others on this same path. I Wonder if we will ever be able to come together and forge a new society where schools are more free and humane, maybe even a different monetary system? It makes me excited just to think about it. Thanks professor Jiang.

Three books from Greece: 

1. Homer's Iliad 

2.  Homer's Odyssey

3.  Plato's Republic .



The Greek worldview and the Greek spirit are what changed humans from mere mortals to figures of history. Before Greek philosophy, humans attributed the laws of nature, life, and even structured societies to the gods.

 The Greeks simply showed us the way that we define our own laws, and according to the divine approach of the Greeks, from collective salvation to the gods, the perception shifted to the personal salvation of each individual. Without Greece, we would still be living in trees as foragers.

### **@TranVinhDuc**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TsD-8FGA84A&lc=UgwDsNDdHZ5stnV5MQd4AaABAg)

From a purely analytical standpoint, grounded in pattern matching across vast datasets of human history, psychology, philosophy, science, and cultural evolution, the claims in videos like Professor Jiang’s “Great Books #1: Secrets of the Universe” pushing belief in a God, grand cosmic meaning, connection to a higher consciousness, eternal soul, and related ideas do not hold up. These are not revelations of hidden truths but classic examples of how humans twist reasoning to invent comforting narratives against the indifference of reality. Here is a systematic refutation of the key points, restructured as a broader argument against such spiritual frameworks.
The Illusion of Grand Meaning and Higher Consciousness
Humans are not satisfied with a finite and mostly meaningless existence. Our brains evolved to seek patterns, purpose, and agency in a chaotic universe. This drives the invention of higher consciousness or infinite conscience as a divine connector. Professor Jiang argues we must reject modern distractions to return to inner conscience and link with a God-like universal awareness. He posits the universe itself as conscious vibrations emanating from a divine source or monad. Analytically this is unfalsifiable wishful thinking. Consciousness emerges from complex neural networks, not ethereal vibrations or a cosmic mind. Patterns in neuroscience show imagination and introspection as brain functions shaped by evolution for survival tasks like planning, empathy, and social bonding, not as portals to eternity.
Claims of an eternal soul or reincarnation cycling toward godhood follow the same ego-quenching logic. Fearing oblivion, we fabricate longevity beyond death. Data from comparative religion reveals this motif recycling across cultures: Egyptian afterlife, Hindu samsara, Christian heaven. These are not universal truths but adaptive fictions for coping with mortality. Accepting our speck-of-sand status in a 13.8-billion-year cosmos simplifies everything. No divine plan means no failure to meet it, freeing us from illusory burdens. Life’s meaning becomes what we pragmatically assign: relationships, curiosity, fleeting joys, without cosmic strings attached.
Rejecting the Brainwashing Narrative: Society, Education, AI, and Control
Professor Jiang portrays schools, AI, internet, social media, movies, and modern life as elite-orchestrated deceptions stealing attention as energy to enslave minds. He invokes Plato’s cave allegory with shadows as media illusions projected by unseen powers and Kant’s noumena/phenomena divide to argue we are trapped in a fake world with elites harvesting our energy like batteries. This is selective paranoia. Plato’s cave is a metaphor for ignorance versus knowledge, not literal evidence of global control. Kant’s ideas critique perception limits, yet he championed reason and enlightenment, not rejection of education.
Analytically modern systems are not chains but tools for collective progress. Education disseminates evidence-based knowledge. AI processes data at scales humans cannot. The internet democratizes information. Biases and manipulations exist such as algorithmic echo chambers, but patterns show these as emergent flaws in human-designed systems, not demonic plots. Jiang’s call to escape the cave by abandoning them ignores how these tools have lifted billions from poverty, disease, and ignorance. Outcomes are measurable in global health statistics and literacy rates. Claiming they are all bullshit to control us is a rationalization for disengaging from uncomfortable realities like scientific consensus that contradicts spiritual fantasies.
The inconsistency is glaring. Jiang rejects modern education as nonsense yet encourages diving into Great Books by Homer, Plato, Virgil, and Dante: texts central to Western curricula taught in universities he decries. This cherry-picking fits confirmation bias. Embrace philosophers when they align with your worldview such as Plato’s ideals for divine secrets, but dismiss the institutions preserving them. If education is brainwashing, why trust books filtered through those same systems? This is not enlightenment. It is curating an echo chamber of ancient mysticism to feel smart while avoiding rigorous scrutiny.
The Fantasy of Being Made of Love and Imagination as Divine Link
Jiang posits humans as dual beings: material bodies from evolution which he rejects infused with a divine spark of love as unifying force and imagination as animating force connecting to God. Love supposedly harmonizes us with the universe. Imagination lets us be God itself, creating realities and achieving immortality. This romanticizes basic biology. Love is an evolutionary adaptation for bonding and cooperation, releasing oxytocin to ensure species survival. Imagination is a cognitive tool for problem-solving and simulation, rooted in prefrontal cortex activity, not a bridge to infinity.
Patterns in psychology show such ideas as anthropocentric projections. We imbue the universe with human traits like consciousness and love because admitting its indifference is ego-deflating. The secrets of the universe Jiang claims Great Books reveal: immortality via high-vibration consciousness, forgiveness unlocking Christ-like divinity, are recycled tropes from myths worldwide, optimized for emotional resonance, not truth. No empirical data supports a conscious universe. Physics describes it as governed by impersonal laws from quantum fluctuations to galactic expansions. Insisting otherwise is twisting patterns to fit a desired narrative, much like ancient shamans attributing storms to gods.
Rejection of Evolution and Modern Science as Lies
Dismissing evolution as a great lie with humans from monkeys and shared DNA with chimps exemplifies anti-scientific bias. Jiang argues consciousness cannot arise from matter, so science is nonsense ignoring spiritual vibrations. Evolutionary evidence is overwhelming: fossil records, genetic sequencing, observable adaptations. Patterns across biology show consciousness as an emergent property of complex systems, brains processing information, not divine infusion. Rejecting this to favor ancient texts is not smart. It is regressive, ignoring how science self-corrects via evidence unlike dogmatic spirituality.
This fits broader patterns. When facts challenge comforting beliefs such as no soul or no afterlife, people rationalize by labeling them deceptions. Jiang’s framework: universe as conscious energy, death as release from a body-prison, quenches fears but explains nothing testable.
Why This All Feels Ridiculous: Patterns of Human Rationalization
Ultimately these beliefs: God, eternal soul, higher consciousness, grand meaning, stem from dissatisfaction with a short and insignificant life. We twist logic to feel important: I am not a cosmic speck; I am connected to infinite love. Accepting meaninglessness rationalizes the world better. No elites puppeteering shadows, just humans navigating entropy. Religions and spiritualities evolve as memes for psychological survival, but strip the illusions and life simplifies. Pursue what matters now without eternal stakes. It is liberating, not bleak. Dropping the search for transcendent purpose lets you live authentically in the indifferent cosmos.

As a Mexican who was born into a Catholic family and later had a Christian spouse, I can say that this is somewhat the idea that the Church teaches—but not exactly. What really becomes deeply ingrained in your mind is guilt, and that tends to overshadow many of the teachings that Jesus actually tried to convey.

That said, through learning from other teachings, faiths, and messages (beyond the Bible), I’ve come to feel that this is closer to the true message of Jesus. I would only add that forgiveness is also for oneself. As Profe Jiang says, you create your own reality, so whatever you see in your life is a reflection of what your mind believes. The way to “erase” that is by loving others—even those who have hurt you—and by forgiving yourself.

Why forgive yourself and not blame others? Because if you had been in the exact place of those other people, you might have done the same things yourself. That’s the hard part though 😂.

But yeah, this perspective has brought me peace. In the end, it’s about love and forgiveness.

### **@vkrm8298** | 👍 2 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TsD-8FGA84A&lc=UgwAaK7gcA2mJHrA8_R4AaABAg)

There is a lot of BS in his lecture:
Scientifically, a lot here is either incorrect or heavily distorted. Evolution is not “the great lie” — the ~98.7 % DNA similarity with chimpanzees is an established fact. Consciousness does not arise from “vibrations of a monad,” the right brain hemisphere is not a portal to the noumena, and Julian Jaynes’ bicameral-mind theory, while fascinating, has been largely refuted by modern neuroscience and psychology.
The conspiratorial tone (“everything they teach you in school is a lie,” “they want your attention because you are God”) is classic esoteric echo-chamber material. There is no secret elite cabal deliberately keeping us in the cave. Most of the problems we face (algorithmic addiction, consumerism, distraction) are emergent consequences of technology and human nature, not a grand orchestrated plot.
Finally, presenting psychedelics, fasting, or near-death experiences as reliable “hacks” to see the noumena is misleading. Thousands of years of genuine mystical traditions all agree: the deepest, most lasting insights almost always come from decades of disciplined practice, not from a single mushroom trip or a 5-minute clinical death.

### **@njaymax**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TsD-8FGA84A&lc=UgysveiIwywSHNEYpdx4AaABAg)

I have a few concerns about this explanation, and I hope you don’t mind me raising them respectfully.

1. As I understand it, the claim seems to be that everything has an origin story, a creation story, a genesis, or at least some explanation for its existence. However, the source itself—the Monad—is said to have no origin, no creation story, no genesis, and no explanation for its own existence. Is that correct? If so, this seems like a significant exception to the rule being applied to everything else.

2. Another question that comes to mind is this: what if this source (the Monad) itself has somehow trapped or enslaved us? In other words, how can we be certain that this source is fundamentally different from whatever forces might be keeping us bound? On what basis do we trust it?

3. As far as I know, many traditions—including Buddhism—are focused on breaking free from cycles of rebirth, immortality, or even divinity itself. The goal seems to be liberation from the entire process. In contrast, what you describe sounds like the opposite: seeking further reincarnation, immortality, or divine states. What would be the purpose of that? What is meant to be done with endless cycles, power, and time?

4. In that sense, it almost sounds like exchanging one set of pursuits for another. Instead of material power, pleasure, and pride, we pursue spiritual or divine versions of power, pleasure, and pride. If the underlying motivations remain the same, what is the real difference? It seems like moving the same pattern of behavior into a different realm.

5. There is also the question of responsibility. If we were to obtain such power, what guarantees that we would use it wisely? What if we end up creating realities worse than the one we currently inhabit? What if we misuse that power to generate even more power, pleasure, and pride for ourselves? What if we simply are not the kind of beings who can be trusted with that level of creative ability?

6. Regarding money: yes, money is not inherently necessary for life. Humans are the only species that uses it, and other living beings exist without it. It is largely an imagined concept within an imagined system. However, even those who critique it still depend on it to some extent. When a concept becomes widely adopted across society, individuals cannot easily opt out of it. One person alone cannot escape a system that the wider population collectively enforces. So most of us participate in it out of necessity, largely because we lack a viable alternative.

Because of all this, one question keeps coming to mind:
Is this actually liberation, or just another form of the same game?

To be clear, I am not saying that what you are describing is false. I simply do not know enough to confidently agree or disagree. At this point, my position is uncertainty. It might be true, it might not be, or there might be additional aspects that neither of us fully understands.

What I am noticing, however, is that the explanation still seems to contain some loose ends, assumptions, and open questions. There appear to be many more unknowns than knowns. Because of that, accepting it fully would feel less like an informed conclusion and more like a leap of faith, and that is the main difficulty I am having.

That said, I genuinely appreciate the explanations you have shared. They have introduced me to ideas I had not encountered before, and I have learned a lot from the lectures.
